The design answer: **the token never enters the LLM's world; a user identifier does.**\n\nFlow (user's framing, 2026-07-09):\n\n1. The user authenticates to **our own service** (the security app).\n2. The service issues a **JWT** for the user.\n3. The user's **UUID and the JWT go into a vault** — the token is stored, not circulated.\n4. From that point on, only the **bare user identifier** is passed around (e.g. into MCP tool-call context).\n5. When a tool actually needs access, a **separate process — the security app, not the tool** — receives the user id, retrieves the token from the vault, retrieves roles, determines what the requested resource allows, and returns **only the applicable roles/capabilities** back to the tool.\n\n**Enforcement principle (inherited from ASR-002):** authorization must gate what the backend *retrieves* (scoped queries), not filter results after the fact based on client-held role information.\n\n**Why statelessness fits:** the adopted MCP mechanism (ASR-003) is Anthropic's native Claude API connector — stateless, no server-side session, per-call authorization only. A stable bare user id as the per-call credential is exactly what a stateless design requires; the vault + separate resolver process supplies the state that the connector can't hold.\n\n## Threat Model — Concrete Credential-Surfacing Paths\n\n(2026-08-18) Names the specific paths \"credentials surfaced\" covers — the gap the ecosystem check flagged (its sources named the exposure-*class*, not this design's specific paths). Each path pairs with the design requirement that defends against it; several of these requirements are now committed in Key Decisions above.\n\n**Assets to protect:** the JWT signing secret; the per-user vault token (the post-auth JWT stored against the user's UUID); any downstream credential the resolver derives on the user's behalf (a scoped DB connection, a third-party API key).\n\n**Trust boundary:** the LLM's context window — conversation history, tool-call arguments, tool-call results, anything that ends up prompt-cached or in a stored transcript — must never contain an asset above. Everything server-side of an MCP tool call (resolver, vault, database) is trusted; anything that crosses into a message Claude reads is not.\n\n| # | Exposure path | Concrete scenario | Defense |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 1 | Tool-result echo | A tool handler bug (or a debug/verbose mode left on) returns the resolved token instead of derived roles/capabilities | `IAuthorizationProvider`'s return type is a typed roles/capabilities model (Pydantic/dataclass) with no token-shaped field — not a dict that could accidentally carry one |\n| 2 | Tool-call argument | A tool signature includes a `user_id` (or worse, a credential) parameter the model fills in — model-visible and model-settable | Don't pass user id as a tool argument at all — resolve it server-side per request from the verified access token before the tool body runs (committed above) |\n| 3 | Confused deputy via #2 | If #2 isn't fixed, a prompt-injected instruction (from blog/document content the model is reasoning over) tricks the model into passing a *different* user id, and the resolver serves another user's data | Same fix as #2 — this is the sharper reason to close it, not just a context-window nicety |\n| 4 | Error/exception bubbling | A vault-lookup or resolver exception's message/stack trace carries token material and becomes (or is embedded in) the tool's error response, which MCP returns as model-readable content | Resolver/vault code catches its own exceptions and translates to a generic, tokenless error before it can become a tool result — never let a raw exception cross that boundary |\n| 5 | Logging | Request/response logging (FastAPI, MCP server, connector-facing endpoint) logs `authorization_token` or vault contents; those logs later surface back to Claude via a log-search or debugging tool | Log user id, never token values — redact `authorization_token` and any vault-returned field at the logging layer itself, not by convention at each call site |\n| 6 | Prompt injection / tool poisoning | Content the model reasons over (a subscribed blog post, an uploaded document) contains an injected instruction trying to get a tool to dump credentials, or to misuse a legitimate tool's parameters | OWASP MCP standard mitigation: narrow tool schemas (no free-text \"run this query\" shape) — and #1–#4 mean there's nothing credential-shaped for an injected instruction to successfully request even if it tries |\n| 7 | Connector-level token visibility | Whether `mcp_servers[].authorization_token` (an API *request* parameter per Anthropic's docs, not a `messages` content block) ever surfaces in the model's context or a stored transcript | Documented as outside the content window by the API's shape — but **verify empirically once the POC has a running connector call**: inspect an actual transcript/prompt-cache entry, don't take the docs' word alone |\n| 8 | State-handle hijacking (MCP spec's term) | MCP is stateless, no protocol-level session; if the resolver's roles/capabilities output were treated as proof of identity, it could be replayed outside the request it was issued for | Bind every resolver output to that request's verified user id server-side; never accept a roles/capabilities value handed back as identity proof — only the verified access token counts |\n| 9 | Persistence / blast radius | If any of #1–#8 leaks a token, it now also lives in whatever stores the conversation (client-side chat history in the researcher/writer UI, prompt cache), extending exposure past the single request | A consequence of getting #1–#8 right, not a separate control — named so it's clear \"don't log it\" alone isn't sufficient once transcripts are persisted |\n\n**Resolves the open question \"how does the user id reach the tool\":** it doesn't reach the tool as a value at all — it's resolved server-side, per request, from the verified access token, invisible to the model. See the corresponding Key Decision above.\n\n## Integration target: `ai-research-blog`\n\n- **Repo:** `/Users/billkratochvil/Dev/repos/ai-research-blog` (`BillKrat/ai-research-blog`) — AI-assisted multi-tenant blog platform: Next.js UI, Python/FastAPI API, PostgreSQL triple store, deployed on Railway (blogresearch.net).\n- **Architecture:** MVPVM pattern (ADR-0002) adapted for request-scoped HTTP; two packages split by reusability — `shared/` (reusable framework) vs `blogresearch/` (app-specific, ADR-0009); small registry-based composition root; **no DI framework, no ORM**.\n- **Most relevant docs — read before designing against this POC:**\n  - `docs/ASR.md` → **ASR-002** (security as the cross-cutting foundation; JWT-based identity; token-in-vault; bare user id across process boundaries) and **ASR-003** (MCP as the tool-layer gateway: one Python MCP server, multiple tools, native Claude API connector, OAuth 2.1 / RFC 8707 for client↔server auth).\n  - `docs/adr/0005-identity-data-in-triple-store.md` — identity/role/group/feature data lives in the **same triple store** as form data (point lookups are fine for triples), accessed via `IUserProvider`; the token-vault resolution is `IAuthorizationProvider`, a deliberately separate service-layer interface.\n  - `docs/adr/0003-provider-interface-split.md` — the one-responsibility-per-interface discipline this codebase follows.\n- **Planned interfaces to align with:** `IUserProvider` (data: role/group/feature triples for a user) and `IAuthorizationProvider` (service: takes user id + resource, resolves credentials, returns roles/capabilities). The *other* branch of the prejudgment did **not** fire: the Claude API MCP connector requires the consumer to supply and refresh the token per call (item 2) — it manages nothing on the user's behalf — so the self-issued JWT + vault + resolver is **not** simplified away. It stays exactly as the 10k-foot design has it, with the resolver's output landing in `mcp_servers[].authorization_token` on each Messages API call.\n\n### Verification results (2026-08-18)\n\n**1. `mcp` Python SDK** — `mcp` **2.0.0** on PyPI (released 2026-07-28): v2 is the current stable line — a major rework built for the **2026-07-28 spec revision** (and every earlier one); v1.x continues on a frozen line receiving security patches only, documented at `py.sdk.modelcontextprotocol.io/v1/`. `FastMCP` is renamed `MCPServer` (import `mcp.server`); `get_context()` is gone — declare a `ctx: Context` parameter instead. **Statelessness: first-class** — the 2026-07-28 spec removes the connection handshake, the session, and every server-initiated request, and `mcp.run(transport=\"streamable-http\", stateless_http=True)` gives \"a fresh transport per request, no session tracking\" (plus `json_response=True`, `event_store`, and `transport_security` for DNS-rebinding protection when deployed off-localhost). **Auth: the resource-server half of OAuth 2.1** — implement `TokenVerifier` (one async method: raw token in → `AccessToken | None` out; the SDK has no opinion on what a valid token is) together with `AuthSettings(issuer_url, resource_server_url, required_scopes)`; the SDK auto-serves RFC 9728 protected-resource metadata at `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/...` and answers unauthenticated requests with a 401 whose `WWW-Authenticate` points at it; the repo ships an RFC 7662 `IntrospectionTokenVerifier` example as the production shape. The legacy embedded authorization server (`auth_server_provider=`) is explicitly not recommended for new servers. **How per-request context reaches handlers:** `get_access_token()` (`mcp.server.auth.middleware.auth_context`) returns the current request's `AccessToken` — `client_id`, `subject`, `scopes`, `expires_at`, plus any extra claims the verifier attached — inside any tool/resource/prompt handler via a per-request context variable; the user UUID rides in as a JWT claim decoded by *our* verifier, with zero plumbing. Caveat: auth exists only on the HTTP transports — over stdio or in memory it is always `None` (the stdio security boundary is the launching process). Sources: pypi.org/project/mcp; `py.sdk.modelcontextprotocol.io` (What's new in v2, Running your server, Authorization); modelcontextprotocol/python-sdk.\n\n**2. Native Claude API MCP connector** — the Messages API \"MCP connector\" feature; current beta header `mcp-client-2025-11-20` (the 2025-04-04 header is deprecated). Available on Claude API, Claude Platform on AWS, and Microsoft Foundry (all beta). **Exact auth mechanics (the design-critical answer):** each `mcp_servers` entry takes `type: \"url\"` (must start with https), `name`, and an optional `authorization_token` — and the official docs state: \"API consumers are expected to handle the OAuth flow and obtain the access token prior to making the API call, and to refresh the token as needed.\" → **The connector carries a per-user token that *we* supply per call; it does NOT do user-scoped OAuth on the user's behalf in the API path.** (The claude.ai *product* connector is a different mechanism — consent-gated OAuth, `oauth_anthropic_creds`, no client_credentials — and is not this POC's target; ASR-003 targets the API.) Constraints that shape the POC: the server must be publicly exposed over HTTP (Streamable HTTP or SSE; no stdio); **only tool calls** travel through the connector (no prompts or resources); multiple servers per request is supported; per-tool allowlist / denylist / `defer_loading` live in `mcp_toolset` objects in the `tools` array; the Batches API accepts `mcp_servers` too; and **MCP-connector data is not covered by ZDR** (standard retention applies — note this when scoping what the tools can return). The in-SDK alternative for local/stdio servers — `anthropic[mcp]` client-side helpers (`anthropic.lib.tools.mcp`) — is ASR-003's \"hand-rolled client/loop in this app's own backend\" option, now a one-import path. Source: platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/mcp-connector; claude.com/docs/connectors/building/authentication.\n\n**3. Published security guidance (feeds the threat-model step)** — the canonical sources found: (a) the **MCP spec's \"Security Best Practices\"** document (2026-07-28 revision; modelcontextprotocol.io) — names, at MUST level: the confused deputy (proxy static client id + consent-cookie theft), **token passthrough (explicitly forbidden — \"MCP servers MUST NOT accept any tokens that were not explicitly issued for the MCP server\"; audience validation per RFC 9068)**, SSRF via OAuth discovery URLs (client side) and via CIMD (server side), **state-handle hijacking** (\"MCP is stateless and has no protocol-level sessions\" — servers MUST NOT treat possession of a state handle as authentication; bind handles server-side to the user id *derived from the verified token*), local MCP server compromise (malicious startup commands, DNS rebinding against localhost), OAuth authorization URL validation (javascript:/shell handling → XSS/RCE), stdio-proxy privilege escalation, mix-up attacks (RFC 9207), localhost redirect impersonation, CIMD trust policies, and progressive least-privilege scope minimization. (b) The **OWASP MCP Security Cheat Sheet** — the prompt-injection / tool-poisoning class plus the 2025 incident record (Asana cross-tenant data exposure, GitHub MCP prompt injection, unauthenticated RCE in Anthropic's own MCP Inspector, npm supply-chain compromises). (c) **Anthropic-specific:** no dedicated Anthropic blog post naming MCP credential exposure paths was found; the closest official guidance is the Claude Code security doc — prompt-injection safeguards (permission system, isolated context windows for web fetch, input sanitization, network-command approval), trust verification for new MCP servers, and the key precedent for this POC's core move: in Anthropic-hosted cloud sessions, \"authentication is handled through a secure proxy that uses a scoped credential inside the sandbox, which is then translated to your actual GitHub authentication token\" — i.e. *a user identifier enters the agent's world; the credential does not*. **Gap to close in the threat-model step:** neither source fully covers the POC's specific concern — a self-issued vault token echoed into model context via tool output, model-visible parameters, or logs. The vault + bare-user-id design is the defense for exactly that; name those paths explicitly in the concrete threat model.\n\n**4. Identity libraries (the \"batteries-included\" candidates)** — `fastapi-users` **15.0.5** (released 2026-03-27): **maintenance mode** — \"security updates and dependency maintenance, no new features\"; the maintainers are building \"a new Python authentication toolkit that will ultimately supersede FastAPI Users\" (unreleased). Feature set otherwise unchanged (register/login/reset/verify routes, social OAuth2, pluggable JWT/database/Redis strategies, SQLAlchemy-async/Beanie). `PyJWT` **2.13.0** (2026-05-21): active, production/stable, trusted publishing with sigstore attestation. `authlib` **1.7.2** (2026-05-06; signed releases; v1.7.0 landed 2026-04): active, full OAuth 2 + OpenID Connect client *and* server, joserfc-based, Python 3.10–3.14. `hvac` **2.4.0** (2025-10-30): active HashiCorp Vault API client, Apache-2.0. **POC selection:** `PyJWT` for the self-issued JWT half — smallest footprint, matches ASR-002's self-contained-JWT lean, and makes no bet on a superseded library; hold `authlib` in reserve if the MCP server ever needs its own interactive authorization server (e.g. for non-API clients); the vault stays a Postgres table behind an interface at POC scale, with `hvac` as the escape hatch if a real vault is wanted.\n\nTwo design consequences to carry into the open questions (both follow from items 1 + 2, not from the POC's own reasoning): (1) the self-issued JWT must be one the MCP server's `TokenVerifier` can validate — audience bound to the MCP server's endpoint, per the spec's token-passthrough rule (RFC 9068); and (2) the backend needs a token-lifecycle path — re-fetch or re-mint from the vault on 401 — because the connector consumes one token per call and the spec's stateless posture gives it nothing else. Also, when scaffolding: pin `mcp>=2.0,<3` (v2 is three weeks old at verification time) and read the migration guide before writing any v1-style (`FastMCP`) code.\n\n## Collaboration & Communication Style\n\n- **User background:** Experienced C# developer, new to Python.\n- **Motivation:** Adopting Python after the new (stateless) Anthropic MCP SDK — with no foreseeable C# support.\n- **Communication:** The user describes requirements and concepts in **C# terms**. Translate the intent, then express it idiomatically in Python.\n- **Do NOT** write \"C# in Python clothing\" — no `foreach`-style habits, verbose property boilerplate, or ported C# patterns. Follow Python best practices in all areas:\n  - PEP 8 style; `snake_case` for variables/functions/modules, `PascalCase` for classes, `UPPER_SNAKE_CASE` for constants\n  - Dataclasses / `TypedDict` over C#-style classes for plain data; `Optional[T]`/`X | None` typing\n  - `pathlib.Path` over raw strings; `with` statements for resources\n  - Comprehensions and generator expressions where readable\n  - `black` formatting, `ruff` linting, `mypy` for type checking (or agreed equivalents)\n  - `pytest` for tests (matches the target project)\n- **Teaching moments:** When a Python idiom replaces a C# pattern the user mentioned, briefly note the correspondence (e.g., \"C# `IEnumerable<T>` + LINQ → Python generator + comprehension\") so the user can learn from the review. Keep notes short; don't over-explain basics an experienced developer doesn't need.\n- **Type hints on everything** public; this is the closest thing to C#'s static typing and helps the user's review.\n- **One exception to pure-Python naming:** the target codebase keeps the C# `I` prefix on interface names (`IUserProvider`, `IAuthorizationProvider`). Match that when using those names; everything else stays conventionally Pythonic.\n\n## Local LM Studio Agent — Role & Workflow\n\n- **Why:** Peak Claude sessions are budget-constrained (usage + provider costs already eat into it). A local model in LM Studio (currently `qwen/qwen3.8-27b` via MLX, with `mcp/filesystem` and — once its MCP config is stable — web search) absorbs legwork between peak sessions, so peak time isn't spent on things a local model can do.\n- **Division of labor:**\n  - **Local LM Studio agent:** repo exploration (`list_directory` / `search_files` / `read_text_file`), the \"Ecosystem Check → Verification list\" research above, drafting scaffolding/docs/code in this POC repo.\n  - **Claude (peak sessions):** design decisions, architecture calls, and the **review/approval gate** — nothing produced here moves into `ai-research-blog` without a Claude session reviewing it first.\n- **Recording results:** when the local agent finishes a verification item or makes a finding, log it in this file (the Key Decisions table, or directly in the relevant table/section it touches) with a date, so the next peak session picks up from there instead of re-deriving it. (Precedent set 2026-08-18: the ecosystem-check verification ran in a Claude session — web search / fetch MCP tools available — and its results were logged the same way.)\n- **Guardrail:** this repo (`poc/`) is the sandbox. Nothing here is \"done\" until a Claude session has reviewed it and deliberately moved it into `ai-research-blog` — the local agent's output is a draft/proposal, not a merge-ready artifact.\n- **Known capability gap (found 2026-08-18, first full scaffolding run) — closed same day.** The local agent's toolset (`mcp/filesystem` + a web-fetch tool + a JS-only sandbox with no shell/`--allow-run` access) had **no way to execute Python or run `pytest`.** It confirmed this itself mid-session (reasoned through and ruled out `run_javascript`/Deno as a path to a shell) and correctly flagged \"not executed yet\" as a caveat in its handoff — that honesty held. But it meant every test file it wrote was composed by reasoning alone, never run, and cross-file contracts it invented in the same sitting (e.g., a fake DB connection's method signature vs. the real driver's shape used elsewhere) could drift without it ever noticing. First run: 31 failed / 12 errors / 22 passed out of ~65 tests, all from this class of bug — see the Key Decisions row above.\n- **`poc-test-runner` MCP tool (added 2026-08-18):** closes the gap above. Two tools, both hardcoded to this repo only — no parameter can point them anywhere else (path-traversal / absolute-path / `~` arguments are rejected before they ever reach a subprocess; source at `~/.lmstudio/tools/poc_test_runner.py`):\n  - `sync_deps()` — runs `uv sync --extra dev` in this repo.\n  - `run_tests(pytest_args=\"\")` — runs `uv run pytest {pytest_args}` in this repo, returns stdout/stderr/returncode. Empty `pytest_args` runs the whole suite; e.g. `\"-k vault\"` or `\"tests/test_vault.py -v\"` scopes it.\n  - **Use it whenever you write or change any code in `src/` or `tests/`: call `sync_deps` once if dependencies might be stale, then `run_tests` after every change, and keep iterating until it's green — or until the tool stops you (see Blocked-Task Protocol below) — before handing back to a peak session.** Don't hand off code you haven't run through this tool — that was exactly the gap that produced the 2026-08-18 failures above.\n  - **`run_tests` has a per-session budget (12 calls / 45 minutes, added 2026-08-18)**, enforced by the tool itself, not by your judgment — it starts warning at call 9 and stops running pytest entirely past call 12 or 45 minutes, returning a message pointing back here. This exists because nothing else bounds how long a fix-and-rerun loop can run once a failure turns out to be genuinely hard, and a long session is exactly where prose instructions like \"stop if you're stuck\" tend to get lost. Budget it accordingly: don't burn 5 calls re-running the whole suite to check one line of a fix — use `pytest_args` to scope to just the failing test(s) while iterating, and only run the full suite once you think you're done or once per few fixes.\n\n  ### Operating discipline (added 2026-08-19, after a session read SDK source for 1500+ lines instead of ever calling the tool)\n\n  The 2026-08-18 capability gap (no way to execute Python) is closed — `poc-test-runner` exists and is reachable. The failure mode now is behavioral, not a missing capability, so it needs a behavioral rule, not another tool:\n\n  - **First tool call, no exceptions:** in any session touching `src/` or `tests/`, the first tool call is `sync_deps` (if dependencies might be stale) followed by a full-suite `run_tests` — before reading any source file, library or otherwise. Get the real, current failure list before doing anything else. Planning to call it later in your own reasoning does not count as having called it — call it first, literally.\n  - **Read library/SDK source only when a specific failing test's traceback names it.** You have an actual error in hand and need to understand the one function/class it points at — that's the only license to open a third-party source file. Never read SDK internals to pre-emptively reason out how a feature \"probably\" behaves; run the test and read what actually happened instead of simulating it in your head.\n  - **Stay inside the assigned scope.** Fix the root causes named in the current task, or whatever `run_tests` actually reports as failing — nothing else. If something else looks interesting while you're in a file, don't chase it: note it in this file under Key Decisions for a peak session to triage later.\n  - **If a tool you expect (`run_tests`, `sync_deps`) isn't in your available tool list this session, say so explicitly in your handoff and stop** — that's a session/config problem for the user to fix, not something to work around by reasoning blind.\n\n  ### Blocked-Task Protocol\n  Triggered when `run_tests` refuses to run (budget exceeded), or on your own judgment if a specific failure has survived 2-3 real fix attempts. Stop touching that test. In this file, under the relevant Key Decisions or Current State entry, record for each test still failing:\n  - **Test name** (e.g. `tests/test_vault.py::test_put_upserts`)\n  - **Exact error/traceback** from your last `run_tests` call — not a paraphrase\n  - **What you tried** — each attempt and why it didn't work\n  - **Your best hypothesis** for the remaining cause, even if unproven\n\n  Then move on: fix what you can elsewhere in the suite, and leave the rest for peak-session review. A partial fix with an honest, detailed write-up on the remainder is far more useful than a full session spent on one stubborn test with nothing to show for it.\n\n## Conventions\n\n- Language: Python 3.12+ (the POC requires `>=3.12`; the target repo runs 3.13.7 — compatible)\n- Styling: `black` + `ruff` (line length 100); types enforced with `mypy` (config in pyproject; untyped third-party deps overridden there)\n- Tests: `pytest`, in `tests/` mirroring source layout. DB-dependent components are tested against an in-memory fake connection via the injectable `connect` parameter (the target repo's pattern) — the suite needs zero infrastructure; `test_integration.py` covers the real-Postgres path and skips unless the POC env vars are set\n- Env/secrets: `.env` (gitignored) + committed `.env.example` with placeholders only — same pattern as the target repo; **never a real secret in a committed file**. Entry points (not library modules) call `load_dotenv()` — the target repo's no-import-time-side-effects discipline\n- Package layout: one flat `src/mcpauth` package (hatchling, src layout) — held loose until the deployment topology is confirmed (docs/0001); module boundaries kept extraction-clean so the port to `ai-research-blog` is a move\n- Naming: the target repo's `I` prefix applies to `IUserProvider` / `IAuthorizationProvider`; everything else conventionally Pythonic (no interface for a single implementation — the vault and identity anchor are concrete on purpose)\n\n## Current State & Next Steps\n\n- [x] Set project context (this file): goal, 10k-foot design, integration target\n- [x] Align with `ai-research-blog` docs (ASR-002/003, ADR-0005)\n- [x] Verify current ecosystem state (see Ecosystem Check) — done 2026-08-18 in a Claude session with web access\n- [x] Concrete threat model — done 2026-08-18, see Threat Model section\n- [x] Decide JWT shape — self-contained, committed 2026-08-18\n- [x] Decide vault implementation at POC scale — Postgres table, committed 2026-08-18\n- [x] Define the identity anchor shape — fixed anchor + triples, committed 2026-08-18\n- [ ] Confirm deployment topology — decision brief in docs/0001 (A/B/C + the tied signing-algorithm decision); recommendation logged 2026-08-18 (in-process resolver); needs user sign-off, tied to MCP-server placement\n- [ ] Enumerate the actual roles/claims list (owner, subscriber, admin?) — the shape is decided, the list isn't; drafted in docs/0002 (pending review)\n- [ ] Define the security app's API contract (what the resolver process exposes) — drafted in docs/0003 (pending review)\n- [x] Set up project scaffolding — done 2026-08-18: pyproject + `src/mcpauth` (single flat package — layout held loose until the deployment topology is confirmed) + 12 test files + docs 0001–0003 + scripts; suite runs on an in-memory fake connection (zero infra), real-Postgres tests skip without the env vars. **Draft — pending review** per the guardrail above\n- [x] **Review the scaffolding** (peak Claude, 2026-08-18): reviewed and **not** approved as-is — see the Key Decisions row above. The docs/0001–0003 design content and AGENTS.md updates the local agent produced are sound and stay; the code+tests need a fix pass before anything moves to `ai-research-blog`\n- [ ] **NEXT TASK for the local LM Studio agent (reissued 2026-08-19 — the 2026-08-18 attempt at this task churned on SDK source for a full session without ever calling the tool; see the 2026-08-19 Key Decisions row and \"Operating discipline\" above. Task itself is unchanged):** fix the test suite, using the `poc-test-runner` MCP tool (`sync_deps` then `run_tests` — see \"Local LM Studio Agent\" section above) to actually verify every fix instead of reasoning blind. **Your first tool call this session must be `sync_deps` then `run_tests`, before reading anything else.** Last known baseline: 31 failed / 12 errors / 22 passed / 1 skipped. At minimum, three known root causes to fix: (a) `tests/conftest.py`'s `_FakeConnection.cursor()` doesn't match real `psycopg2`'s two-step `cursor()` → `cur.execute(sql, params)` shape that `src/mcpauth` actually uses; (b) `test_auth_app.py` builds a sync `httpx.Client` over `ASGITransport`, which is async-only — switch to `httpx.AsyncClient` + `pytest-asyncio` (or a sync `TestClient`); (c) `test_resolver.py` references a `_logged_in_user` fixture that doesn't exist in `conftest.py` — add it or fix whatever the test meant to reference. There may be more once the suite actually runs — use `run_tests` after every change and keep iterating until the whole suite is green, not just these three. If you hit the tool's per-session budget or get genuinely stuck on a specific test, follow the Blocked-Task Protocol above rather than continuing to guess. Log what you found/fixed (and anything left blocked, per that protocol) in this table with today's date when done. Leave it as a draft for peak-session review either way (guardrail above still applies — passing tests make it a better draft, not an auto-approved one)\n- [ ] **Review docs 0001–0003** (peak Claude per the guardrail): the roles/claims draft (docs/0002) and the security-app API (docs/0003) are the substantive design work\n- [ ] **Empirical check of threat path #7**: run `scripts/connector_sim.py` against a live server with a real Anthropic key; inspect an actual transcript/prompt-cache entry for `authorization_token` — the docs say it stays outside the content window, verify rather than trust\n- [ ] **Scoped-retrieval tool demo**: one stub tool that pushes the user's grants into the query (the ASR-002 enforcement principle — authorization gates what the backend *retrieves*, not a post-filter)\n- [ ] Extraction into `ai-research-blog` (after review + topology): follow that repo's `shared/` vs `blogresearch/` split and ADR conventions\n\n## Reference\n\n- This repo: `/Users/billkratochvil/Dev/repos/poc`\n- Target project: `/Users/billkratochvil/Dev/repos/ai-research-blog` — start with its `AGENTS.md`, `README.md`, `docs/ASR.md` (ASR-002/003), `docs/adr/0005` and `docs/adr/0003`\n"}]

_dispatch(self, sql: str, params: tuple) -> None:\n        if sql.startswith(\"SELECT\"):\n            if \"FROM identity_anchors WHERE email\" in sql:\n                self._rows = [row for row in Returns the user id, or None on any failure.\n\n        One generic failure mode on purpose: unknown email and wrong\n        password are indistinguishable to the caller (no user enumeration).\n        \"\"\"\n        with closing(self._connect(self._conn_string)) as conn:\n            with conn.cursor() as cur:\n                cur.execute(\n                    \"SELECT id, password_hash FROM identity_anchors WHERE email = %s\",\n                    (email,),\n                )\n                row = cur.fetchone()\n        if row is None:\n            return None\n        try:\n            self._hasher.verify(row[1], password)\n        except Exception:\n            # argon2 raises on mismatch or a corrupt hash — both mean \"no\"\n            return None\n        return str(row[0])\n\n\n---\n/Users/billkratochvil/Dev/repos/poc/src/mcpauth/user_provider.py:\n\"\"\"Postgres-backed `IUserProvider`: roles as identity triples.\n\nAGENTS.md (2026-08-18): roles/groups/memberships as triples referencing\nthe anchor user id; already granted — idempotent no-op\n            conn.rollback()\n\n\n---\n/Users/billkratochvil/Dev/repos/poc/src/mcpauth/db.py:\n\"\"\"Shared connection plumbing: `DATABASE_URL` + schema init.\n\nOne connection string for the whole POC, as in the target repo (its\nPostgres repositories all read the same `DATABASE_URL`).\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport os\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\n# <repo>/scripts/schema.sql — this file lives at <repo>/src/mcpauth.\n_SCHEMA_FILE = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2] / \"scripts\" / \"schema.sql\"\n\n\ndef resolve_conn_string(conn_string: str | None = None) -> str:\n    \"\"\"An explicit conn string, else `DATABASE_URL` from the environment.\"\"\"\n    conn_string = conn_string or os.environ.get(\"DATABASE_URL\")\n    if not conn_string:\n        raise ValueError(\"No connection string: pass one or set DATABASE_URL (.env).\")\n    return conn_string\n\n\ndef init_schema(conn_string: str | None = None) -> None:\n    \"\"\"Create the POC tables if absent (idempotent — see scripts/schema.sql).\"\"\"\n    import psycopg2\n\n    with psycopg2.connect(resolve_conn_string(conn_string)) as conn:\n        with conn.cursor() as cur:\n            cur.execute(_SCHEMA_FILE.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8\"))\n        conn.commit()\n\n\n---\n/Users/billkratochvil/Dev/repos/poc/src/mcpauth/resolver.py:\n\"\"\"`IAuthorizationProvider` — the novel, custom part of this POC.\n\nThere is no off-the-shelf per-user token resolver for a multi-tenant MCP\nserver (AGENTS.md ecosystem check) — this is the one custom component;\nthe standard stack (mcp SDK's OAuth 2.1 verification, PyJWT) composes\naround it.\n\nPer-call flow (AGENTS.md 10k-foot view, step 5):\n  1. Take a bare user id — resolved server-side from the verified token,\n     never a tool parameter (threat model paths #2/#3).\n  2. Fetch the stored JWT from the vault. Missing → deny. This is also\n     the POC's pre-expiry revocation point: a deleted vault entry\n     invalidates the presented token before its JWT expires.\n  3. Re-verify the stored token (signature/issuer/audience/expiry) and\n     read its `roles` claim — roles ride in the token (committed\n     self-contained decision); they were looked up at *mint* time.\n  4. Map roles → grants for the requested resource (draft matrix,\n     docs/0002). Unknown roles grant nothing — fail closed.\n  5. Return the typed `AuthorizationResult` — the only shape that crosses\n     into a tool result. Never a credential, never a raw claims dict\n     (threat model path #1).\n\nAll failure paths raise `AuthorizationError`-family exceptions whose\n`str()` is a generic, tokenless message (threat model path #4).\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport logging\n\nfrom mcpauth.capabilities import AuthorizationResult, CapabilityGrant\nfrom mcpauth.errors import AuthorizationError, InvalidStoredTokenError, NoTokenStoredError\nfrom mcpauth.interfaces import IAuthorizationProvider\nfrom mcpauth.jwt_service import decode_or_none\n\nlogger = logging.getLogger(__name__)\n\n# Draft roles/claims matrix — docs/0002 (pending review).\n#   role → resource → actions\n# Resource names follow the planned tools (ASR-003/ASR-004): blogs,\n# documents, subscriptions.\nROLE_GRANTS: dict[str, dict[str, list[str]]] = {\n    \"admin\": {\n        \"blogs\": [\"read\", \"write\", \"manage\"],\n        \"documents\": [\"read\", \"write\", \"manage\"],\n        \"subscriptions\": [\"read\", \"write\"],\n    },\n    \"owner\": {\n        \"blogs\": [\"read\", \"write\"],\n        \"documents\": [\"read\", \"write\"],\n    },\n}\n\n# Resource key meaning \"every resource this role grants anything on\".\nWILDCARD = \"*\"\n\n\nclass PostgresAuthorizationProvider(IAuthorizationProvider):\n    \"\"\"Vault + the self-contained JWT's claims → typed grants.\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, vault: object, secret: str, issuer: str, audience: str) -> None:\n        # `vault` is structurally a token vault (a `.get(user_id)` method).\n        # Typed `object` rather than an interface: one implementation at\n        # POC scale; the seam appears if the backend does (docs/0001).\n        self._vault = vault\n        self._secret = secret\n        self._issuer = issuer\n        self._audience = audience\n\n    def authorize(self, user_id: str, resource: str) -> AuthorizationResult:\n        stored = self._vault_get(user_id)\n        if stored is None:\n            # user id is not an asset (the token is) — safe to log\n            logger.info(\"authorization denied: no vault entry for user %s (unknown user or revoked)\", user_id)\n            raise NoTokenStoredError()\n        payload = decode_or_none(stored, secret=self._secret, issuer=self._issuer, audience=self._audience)\n        if payload is None:\n            logger.warning(\"authorization denied: stored token for user %s failed verification\", user_id)\n            raise InvalidStoredTokenError()\n        roles = payload.get(\"roles\")\n        if not isinstance(roles, list):\n            raise InvalidStoredTokenError()\n\n        grants: list[CapabilityGrant] = []\n        for role in roles:\n            if not isinstance(role, str):\n                continue\n            grant = self._grants_for(role, resource)\n            if grant is not None:\n                grants.append(grant)\n        return AuthorizationResult(user_id=user_id, resource=resource, grants=grants)\n\n    def _vault_get(self, user_id: str) -> str | None:\n        try:\n            return self._vault.get(user_id)  # type: ignore[attr-defined]\n        except Exception as exc:\n            # Threat model path #4: a raw vault/DB error must never cross\n            # into a tool result. Log the exception *class* (a DB error\n            # string may quote SQL parameters); never its message here.\n            logger.error(\"vault lookup failed for user %s: %s\", user_id, exc.__class__.__name__)\n            raise AuthorizationError(detail=f\"vault lookup failed: {exc.__class__.__name__}\") from exc\n\n    def _grants_for(self, role: str, resource: str) -> CapabilityGrant | None:\n        table = ROLE_GRANTS.get(role)\n        if table is None:\n            return None  # unknown role → no grants (fail closed)\n        if resource == WILDCARD:\n            actions = sorted({action for granted in table.values() for action in granted})\n        else:\n            actions = table.get(resource)\n        if not actions:\n            return None\n ### Assistant
Now I understand the failure:

**Root cause 1 — Fake cursor shape.** `_FakeConnection.cursor(sql, params)` takes 2 args, but the real psycopg2 is 2-stage: `conn.cursor()` takes no args → `cur.execute(sql, params)`. Fix: rewrite the fake to match psycopg2's 2-stage shape:

```python
class _FakeCursor:
    def __init__(self, db):
        self._db = db
        self._rows = []

    def execute(self, sql, params):
        self._dispatch(" ".join(sql.split()), params)

    ...
class _FakeConnection:
    def cursor(self):  # no args, matches psycopg2
        return _FakeCursor(self._db)
```

But note: `FakeDb.execute(sql, params)` is "for direct seeding/inspection from tests" — currently it runs `_FakeCursor(self, sql, params)._rows` right away. _bearer_token(request: Request) -> str | None:\n    header = request.headers.get(\"authorization\", \"\")\n    if not header.lower().startswith(\"bearer \"):\n        return None\n    token = header[7:].strip()\n    return token or None\n\n\ndef main() -> None:\n    import uvicorn\n\n    from dotenv import load_dotenv\n\n    load_dotenv()\n    settings = Settings.from_env()\n auth: `AuthSettings` + our\n`TokenVerifier` (`PyJwtTokenVerifier`). `stateless_http=True` matches\nASR-003's stateless Claude API connector: a fresh transport per request,\nno session, per-call authorization. `json_response=True` for the local\nconnector sim (switch per your consumer's transport preference — either\nshape satisfies the MCP spec).\n\n**The user id never appears as a tool parameter** (AGENTS.md key decision\n2026-08-18; threat model paths #2/#3): the tool resolves it server-side,\nper request, from the verified access token — `get_access_token().subject`\n— before its body runs. The model has no user id to set, so a\nprompt-injected instruction cannot hand the resolver someone else's.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport asyncio\nimport logging\nfrom typing import Literal\n\nfrom mcp.server import MCPServer\nfrom mcp.server.auth.middleware.auth_context import get_access_token\nfrom mcp.server.auth.settings import AuthSettings\n\nfrom mcpauth.capabilities import AuthorizationResult\nfrom mcpauth.config import Settings\nfrom mcpauth.errors import AuthorizationError\nfrom mcpauth.interfaces import IAuthorizationProvider\nfrom mcpauth.resolver import WILDCARD, PostgresAuthorizationProvider\nfrom mcpauth.token_verifier import PyJwtTokenVerifier\nfrom mcpauth.vault import PostgresTokenVault\n\nlogger = logging.getLogger(__name__)\n\n# A closed set, not free text — the OWASP MCP cheat sheet's \"narrow tool\n# schemas\" mitigation (threat model path #6): no \"run this query\" shape.\nResource = Literal[\"blogs\", \"documents\", \"subscriptions\", WILDCARD]\n\n\ndef build_server(\n    settings: Settings,\n    *,\n    resolver: IAuthorizationProvider | None = None,\n    vault: PostgresTokenVault | None = None,\n) -> MCPServer:\n    \"\"\"The MCP server with the full per-user authorization chain.\n\n    Dependencies are injectable (tests pass a fake-backed vault); defaults\n    build the Postgres-backed ones.\n    \"\"\"\n    vault = vault if vault is not None else PostgresTokenVault(conn_string=settings.database_url)\n    authorization = (\n        resolver\n        if resolver is not None\n        else PostgresAuthorizationProvider(\n            vault=vault,\n            secret=settings.jwt_secret,\n            issuer=settings.jwt_issuer,\n            audience=settings.mcp_server_url,\n        )\n    )\n\n    server = MCPServer(\n        name=\"blogresearch-mcp\",\n        description=\"Blogresearch tools, per-user authorized (POC).\",\n        token_verifier=PyJwtTokenVerifier(\n            secret=settings.jwt_secret,\n            issuer=settings.jwt_issuer,\n            audience=settings.mcp_server_url,\n        ),\n        auth=AuthSettings(\n            issuer_url=settings.jwt_issuer,\n            resource_server_url=settings.mcp_server_url,\n            # No required scopes for the POC: the verified `sub` (user) is\n            # the control. If tools differentiate by permission later,\n            # scopes become the coarser first cut.\n            required_scopes=None,\n        ),\n    )\n\n    @server.tool(\n        name=\"get_my_capabilities\",\n        description=(\n            \"List the capabilities (roles and actions) the authenticated user holds for a \"\n            \"resource. Returns roles/capabilities only — never a credential. The caller's \"\n            \"identity is taken from the verified access token, not from any parameter.\"\n        ),\n    )\n    async def get_my_capabilities(resource: Resource = WILDCARD) -> AuthorizationResult:\n        token = get_access_token()\n        if token is None or token.subject is None:\n            # Unreachable over authenticated HTTP (RequireAuthMiddleware\n            # 401s first) — but the tool must fail closed if it is ever\n            # reached unauthenticated (e.g. over stdio, where the SDK's\n            # auth context is always None).\n            raise AuthorizationError(detail=\"no verified access token in request context\")\n        try:\n            # The resolver is sync (psycopg2) — hop to a thread so it\n            # doesn't block the event loop. (The target repo is sync-first;\n            # the async seam stays at the edge.)\n            return await asyncio.to_thread(authorization.authorize, token.subject, resource)\n        except AuthorizationError:\n            # Becomes a CallToolResult(is_error=True) whose text is the\n            # generic message — never token material (threat model #4).\n            raise\n\n    return server\n\n\ndef main() -> None:\n    from dotenv import load_dotenv\n\n    load_dotenv()\n    settings = Settings.from_env()\n    server = build_server(settings)\n    server.run(\n        transport=\"streamable-http\",\n        host=settings.mcp_host,\n        port=settings.mcp_port,\n        streamable_http_path=\"/mcp\",\n        json_response=settings.mcp_json_response,\n        stateless_http=True,\n    )\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n\n\n---\n/Users/billkratochvil/Dev/repos/poc/pyproject.toml:\n[project]\nname = \"mcpauth\"\nversion = \"0.1.0\"\ndescription = \"POC: secure per-user identity/authorization for the MCP tool layer of ai-research-blog (ASR-002/003)\"\nreadme = \"README.md\"\nrequires-python = \">=3.12\"\ndependencies = [\n    # The standard stack (AGENTS.md ecosystem check): the official SDK does\n    # client<->server auth; PyJWT does the self-issued JWT. The situation has become clear. All 4 root causes can be confirmed from the traceback and source:

1. **conftest fake cursor**: `_FakeConnection.cursor(sql, params)` (one step) vs. the actual psycopg2-style two-step `conn.cursor()` → `cur.execute(sql, params)` used in `vault.py`/`identity.py`/`user_provider.py`. This is what's causing the failures in `test_vault` (5), `test_identity` (6), `test_user_provider` (1), `test_resolver` (3), and the 8 errors in `test_mcp_server` (the fixture dies at `vault.put`).
2. **`test_user_provider`**: it calls `add_role(..., connect=fake_connect)` without `conn_string` → `resolve_conn_string(None)` raises an exception (since there's no `.env`, `DATABASE_URL` isn't set). The sibling helper is already passing `conn_string="unused"` — it's just that the test is omitting it.
3. **`test_resolver`**: `_logged_in_user` is a regular function, but 4 tests request it as a fixture → add `@pytest.fixture`.
4. **`test_auth_app` (11)**: a sync `httpx.Client` over an async-only `ASGITransport`. Also the same pattern in `test_mcp_server`. AGENTS.md's option (b) proposes `httpx.AsyncClient` + pytest-asyncio (a new dependency + async-ifying 19 tests), or "a sync `TestClient`" — the minimal, non-invasive fix is fastapi's `TestClient` (a subclass of `httpx.Client`, so the type annotations keep working as-is).

Excellent progress! 24 passing, 1 failing: `test_verify_correct_password_returns_id` — `verify` returns None.

Why? Inside `identity.verify`:
```python
cur.execute("SELECT id, password_hash FROM identity_anchors WHERE email = %s", (email,))
row = cur.fetchone()
...
self._hasher.verify(row[1], password)
```
The dispatch for the fake: `"FROM identity_anchors WHERE email" in sql` → `self._rows = [row for row in self._db.anchors if row[1] == params[0]]`. The anchors are stored as a 3-tuple `(anchor_id, email, password_hash)`. `fetchone()` returns the full 3-tuple → `row[1]` is **email**, not password_hash! `self._hasher.verify(email, password)` → argon2 exception → None.

The SQL is `SELECT id, password_hash ...` — the fake is returning the whole row including email at index 1. In real postgres, row = (id, password_hash), so row[1] = hash. Mismatch: the fake's dispatch returns the complete anchor tuple rather than projecting the requested columns.

Fix in conftest: for the `SELECT id, password_hash FROM identity_anchors WHERE email` case, project: `self._rows = [(row[0], row[2]) for row in self._db.anchors if row[1] == params[0]]`.

But the conftest docstring says "FakeDb matches the *exact* SQL strings that the component issues" — the dispatch keys off SQL substrings. Currently it ignores which columns were selected. Minimal fix: project the two selected columns in that branch.

Are there any other tests that rely on anchors rows via the fake? test_verify_corrupt_hash sets `fake_db.anchors[0] = (id, email, "not-a-real-hash")` — no problem.

Any other column-projection mismatches? vault get: `SELECT jwt FROM token_vault WHERE user_id` → dispatch returns `[(jwt,)]` — correct projection. triple_store select: `SELECT object_value FROM triple_store WHERE subject AND predicate` → returns `[(object_value,)]` — correct. identity select is the only one that's not projecting.

Fix that branch. Then, rerun test_identity.py (in a scoped way) + a quick rerun of the 4-file set? Run 3: `tests/test_identity.py -q`.

&lt;/think&gt;


24/25 pass now. The one real bug left: `identity.verify` runs `SELECT id, password_hash ...` and reads `row[1]` as the hash, but the fake returns the full 3-tuple `(id, email, password_hash)` — so `row[1]` is the email, argon2 verification fails, and `verify` returns `None`. Real postgres would project the two requested columns. The fake needs to project too:

```
RuntimeError: Task group is not initialized. Make sure to use run().
```

The SDK's `StreamableHTTPSessionManager` requires `await session_manager.run()` to be active while requests are being processed. This is normally done inside the app's **lifespan**, and Starlette's `TestClient` only runs the ASGI lifespan when used as a context manager (`with TestClient(...) as client:`) — the fixtures build the client and return it, but don't enter the context. Consider deriving capabilities\n    # entirely from server state (e.g. constructor params for list_changed) instead of\n    # requiring callers to assemble them at create_initialization_options() time.\n    def create_initialization_options(\n        self,\n        notification_options: NotificationOptions | None = None,\n        experimental_capabilities: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] | None = None,\n        extensions: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] | None = None,\n    ) -> InitializationOptions:\n        \"\"\"Create initialization options from this server instance.\n\n        `extensions` advertises SEP-2133 extension support under\n        `ServerCapabilities.extensions`; keys are extension identifiers (e.g.\n        `io.modelcontextprotocol/ui`), values are per-extension settings.\n        Defaults to `self.extensions`, which higher layers populate.\n        \"\"\"\n        return InitializationOptions(\n            server_name=self.name,\n            server_version=self.version,\n            title=self.title,\n            description=self.description,\n            capabilities=self.get_capabilities(\n                notification_options or NotificationOptions(),\n                experimental_capabilities or {},\n                extensions if extensions is not None else self.extensions,\n            ),\n            instructions=self.instructions,\n            website_url=self.website_url,\n            icons=self.icons,\n        )\n\n    def get_capabilities(\n        self,\n        notification_options: NotificationOptions | None = None,\n        experimental_capabilities: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] | None = None,\n        extensions: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] | None = None,\n        *,\n        protocol_version: str | None = None,\n    ) -> types.ServerCapabilities:\n        \"\"\"Convert existing handlers to a ServerCapabilities object.\n\n        `extensions` is the SEP-2133 extension map (identifier -> settings)\n        advertised under `ServerCapabilities.extensions`; it defaults to\n        `self.extensions`.\n\n        `protocol_version` makes the subscription-delivered bits era-honest:\n        at 2026-07-28+ versions, change notifications are delivered only on\n        `subscriptions/listen` streams, so the `listChanged` flags and\n        `resources.subscribe` derive from whether that method is served -\n        `notification_options` and the legacy `resources/subscribe` handler\n        (which the modern wire cannot dispatch) are ignored. When omitted, the\n        handshake-era derivation applies unchanged.\n        \"\"\"\n        notification_options = notification_options or NotificationOptions()\n        prompts_capability = None\n        resources_capability = None\n        tools_capability = None\n        logging_capability = None\n        completions_capability = None\n\n        if protocol_version in MODERN_PROTOCOL_VERSIONS:\n            listen_served = \"subscriptions/listen\" in self._request_handlers\n            prompts_changed = tools_changed = resources_changed = subscribe = listen_served\n        else:\n            prompts_changed = notification_options.prompts_changed\n            tools_changed = notification_options.tools_changed\n            resources_changed = notification_options.resources_changed\n            subscribe = \"resources/subscribe\" in self._request_handlers\n\n        # Set prompt capabilities if handler exists\n        if \"prompts/list\" in self._request_handlers:\n            prompts_capability = types.PromptsCapability(list_changed=prompts_changed)\n\n        # Set resource capabilities if handler exists\n        if \"resources/list\" in self._request_handlers:\n            resources_capability = types.ResourcesCapability(\n                subscribe=subscribe,\n                list_changed=resources_changed,\n            )\n\n        # Set tool capabilities if handler exists\n        if \"tools/list\" in self._request_handlers:\n            tools_capability = types.ToolsCapability(list_changed=tools_changed)\n\n        # Set logging capabilities if handler exists\n        if \"logging/setLevel\" in self._request_handlers:\n            logging_capability = types.LoggingCapability()\n\n        # Set completions capabilities if handler exists\n        if \"completion/complete\" in self._request_handlers:\n            completions_capability = types.CompletionsCapability()\n\n        capabilities = types.ServerCapabilities(\n            prompts=prompts_capability,\n            resources=resources_capability,\n            tools=tools_capability,\n            logging=logging_capability,\n            experimental=experimental_capabilities,\n            extensions=extensions if extensions is not None else (self.extensions or None),\n            completions=completions_capability,\n        )\n        return capabilities\n\n    @property\n    def server_info(self) -> types.Implementation:\n        \"\"\"The `serverInfo` block describing this implementation.\n\n        Derived from the constructor's identity fields. An unversioned server\n        reports an empty `version`; the SDK never substitutes its own.\n        \"\"\"\n        return types.Implementation(\n            name=self.name,\n            version=self.version,\n            title=self.title,\n            description=self.description,\n            website_url=self.website_url,\n            icons=self.icons,\n        )\n\n    @cached_property\n    def _server_info_stamp_source(self) -> dict[str, Any]:\n        # Identity is fixed at construction, so the dump is computed once per\n        # server instead of per request. Never handed out directly: nested\n        # values (`icons`) would alias the cache into stamped responses.\n        return self.server_info.model_dump(by_alias=True, mode=\"json\", exclude_none=True)\n\n    @property\n    def server_info_stamp(self) -> dict[str, Any]:\n        \"\"\"A fresh wire dump of `server_info`; callers own the returned dict.\n\n        Each access materializes a deep copy of the once-per-server dump, so\n        a caller mutating a stamped response can never corrupt the identity\n        stamped into later responses.\n        \"\"\"\n        return copy.deepcopy(self._server_info_stamp_source)\n\n    async def _handle_discover(\n        self, ctx: ServerRequestContext[LifespanResultT], params: types.RequestParams | None\n    ) -> types.DiscoverResult:\n        \"\"\"Default `server/discover` handler.\n\n        Auto-derived from server state at call time, so capabilities reflect\n        whatever has been registered (constructor `on_*` kwargs and later\n        `add_request_handler` calls). Operators can replace it wholesale via\n        `add_request_handler(\"server/discover\", ...)`. Reachability for legacy\n        peers is decided at the boundary (`types.methods`), not here.\n        \"\"\"\n        return types.DiscoverResult(\n            supported_versions=list(MODERN_PROTOCOL_VERSIONS),\n            capabilities=self.get_capabilities(protocol_version=ctx.protocol_version),\n            instructions=self.instructions,\n        )\n\n    @property\n    def session_manager(self) -> StreamableHTTPSessionManager:\n        \"\"\"Get the StreamableHTTP session manager.\n\n        Raises:\n            RuntimeError: If called before streamable_http_app() has been called.\n        \"\"\"\n        if self._session_manager is None:\n            raise RuntimeError(  # pragma: no cover\n                \"Session manager can only be accessed after calling streamable_http_app(). \"\n                \"The session manager is created lazily to avoid unnecessary initialization.\"\n            )\n        return self._session_manager\n\n    async def run(\n        self,\n        read_stream: ReadStream[SessionMessage | Exception],\n        write_stream: WriteStream[SessionMessage],\n        initialization_options: InitializationOptions,\n        # When False, exceptions are returned as messages to the client.\n        # When True, exceptions are raised, which will cause the server to shut down\n        # but also make tracing exceptions much easier during testing and when using\n        # in-process servers.\n        raise_exceptions: bool = False,\n    ) -> None:\n        \"\"\"Serve a single connection over the given streams until the read side closes.\n\n        Thin wrapper over `serve_dual_era_loop`: enters the server lifespan,\n        then drives the loop, serving the legacy handshake era and the modern\n        per-request-envelope era (the client's first request decides which).\n        Transports with their own lifespan owner (the streamable-HTTP manager)\n        call `serve_loop` directly instead.\n        \"\"\"\n        async with self.lifespan(self) as lifespan_context:\n            await serve_dual_era_loop(\n                self,\n                read_stream,\n                write_stream,\n                lifespan_state=lifespan_context,\n                init_options=initialization_options,\n                raise_exceptions=raise_exceptions,\n            )\n\n    def streamable_http_app(\n        self,\n        *,\n        streamable_http_path: str = \"/mcp\",\n        json_response: bool = False,\n        stateless_http: bool = False,\n        event_store: EventStore | None = None,\n        retry_interval: int | None = None,\n        max_request_body_size: int = DEFAULT_MAX_REQUEST_BODY_SIZE,\n        transport_security: TransportSecuritySettings | None = None,\n        host: str = \"127.0.0.1\",\n        auth: AuthSettings | None = None,\n        token_verifier: TokenVerifier | None = None,\n        auth_server_provider: OAuthAuthorizationServerProvider[Any, Any, Any] | None = None,\n        custom_starlette_routes: list[Route] | None = None,\n        debug: bool = False,\n    ) -> Starlette:\n        \"\"\"Return an instance of the StreamableHTTP server app.\"\"\"\n        # Auto-enable DNS rebinding protection for localhost (IPv4 and IPv6)\n        if transport_security is None and host in (\"127.0.0.1\", \"localhost\", \"::1\"):\n            transport_security = TransportSecuritySettings(\n                enable_dns_rebinding_protection=True,\n                allowed_hosts=[\"127.0.0.1:*\", \"localhost:*\", \"[::1]:*\"],\n                allowed_origins=[\"http://127.0.0.1:*\", \"http://localhost:*\", \"http://[::1]:*\"],\n            )\n\n        session_manager = StreamableHTTPSessionManager(\n            app=self,\n            event_store=event_store,\n            retry_interval=retry_interval,\n            json_response=json_response,\n            stateless=stateless_http,\n            security_settings=transport_security,\n            max_request_body_size=max_request_body_size,\n        )\n        self._session_manager = session_manager\n\n        # Create the ASGI handler\n        streamable_http_app = StreamableHTTPASGIApp(session_manager)\n\n        # Create routes\n        routes: list[Route | Mount] = []\n        middleware: list[Middleware] = []\n        required_scopes: list[str] = []\n\n        # Set up auth if configured\n        if auth:\n            required_scopes = auth.required_scopes or []\n\n            # Add auth middleware if token verifier is available\n            if token_verifier:\n                middleware = [\n                    Middleware(\n                        AuthenticationMiddleware,\n                        backend=BearerAuthBackend(token_verifier),\n                    ),\n                    Middleware(AuthContextMiddleware),\n                ]\n\n            # Add auth endpoints if auth server provider is configured\n            if auth_server_provider:\n                routes.extend(\n                    create_auth_routes(\n                        provider=auth_server_provider,\n                        issuer_url=auth.issuer_url,\n                        service_documentation_url=auth.service_documentation_url,\n                        client_registration_options=auth.client_registration_options,\n                        revocation_options=auth.revocation_options,\n                        identity_assertion_enabled=auth.identity_assertion_enabled,\n                    )\n                )\n\n        # Set up routes with or without auth\n        if token_verifier:\n            # Determine resource metadata URL\n            resource_metadata_url = None\n            if auth and auth.resource_server_url:  # pragma: no branch\n                # Build compliant metadata URL for WWW-Authenticate header\n                resource_metadata_url = build_resource_metadata_url(auth.resource_server_url)\n\n            routes.append(\n                Route(\n                    streamable_http_path,\n                    endpoint=RequireAuthMiddleware(streamable_http_app, required_scopes, resource_metadata_url),\n                )\n            )\n        else:\n            # Auth is disabled, no wrapper needed\n            routes.append(\n                Route(\n                    streamable_http_path,\n                    endpoint=streamable_http_app,\n                )\n            )\n\n        # Add protected resource metadata endpoint if configured as RS\n        if auth and auth.resource_server_url:\n            routes.extend(\n                create_protected_resource_routes(\n                    resource_url=auth.resource_server_url,\n                    authorization_servers=[auth.issuer_url],\n                    scopes_supported=auth.required_scopes,\n                )\n            )\n\n        if custom_starlette_routes:\n            routes.extend(custom_starlette_routes)\n\n        return Starlette(\n            debug=debug,\n            routes=routes,\n            middleware=middleware,\n            lifespan=lambda app: session_manager.run(),\n        )\n"}]

An exception raised deep in the resolver becomes a tool result\nthe model reads; a raw exception message can quote SQL parameters — and\nSQL parameters can contain the token (e.g. a vault lookup that fails on a\nconstraint quoting the value).\n\nTwo layers, enforced structurally:\n  * `detail` — the internal context. Stays in server logs; a\n    `logging_utils.redact()` pass scrubs token-shaped strings from there.\n  * `public_message` — the *only* text that may cross a user/model\n    boundary. A fixed, generic string per error class.\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\n\nclass AuthorizationError(Exception):\n    \"\"\"Base for every authorization-layer failure.\"\"\"\n\n    public_message = \"Authorization failed. No further details.\"\n\n    def __init__(self, detail: str | None = None) -> None:\n        # Exception's args carry the *public* message: `str(exc)` (which is\n        # how an SDK renders a tool error) must never be able to reach the\n        # detail — even if a caller constructs one with raw material.\n        super().__init__(self.public_message)\n        self.detail = detail or self.public_message\n\n    def __str__(self) -> str:\n        return self.public_message\n\n\nclass NoTokenStoredError(AuthorizationError):\n    \"\"\"No vault entry for this user: unknown user, or the session was\n    revoked (vault entry deleted).\"\"\"\n\n    public_message = \"No active session for this user.\"\n\n\nclass InvalidStoredTokenError(AuthorizationError):\n    \"\"\"A vault entry exists but the stored token fails verification\n    (signature/issuer/audience/expiry) or is malformed.\"\"\"\n\n    public_message = \"Stored session is invalid.\"\n\n\nclass BadCredentialsError(Exception):\n    \"\"\"Security app register/login failures. One generic message by\n    design: unknown email and wrong password must be indistinguishable to\n    the caller (no user enumeration).\"\"\"\n\n    public_message = \"Invalid email or password.\"\n\n    def __str__(self) -> str:\n        return self.public_message\n"}]
